MeshLink routing simulation — stage A: who should a phone connect to?

Every number on this page is simulated. It comes from a simplified computer model of phones and Bluetooth links, calibrated against a few lab measurements. It is not a measurement on real devices.

The question

MeshLink phones talk to each other over Bluetooth Low Energy. A phone can hold only a few live connections at once (its degree budget d, here 3 to 8). Messages travel hop by hop over these connections. So each phone must choose which nearby phones to connect to. Good choices give one big, well-connected mesh in which messages need few hops. Poor choices give isolated clusters that cannot reach each other although they are in radio range.

Stage A compares two ways of choosing (the others come in later stages):

  • N0 — strongest signal: keep the ~¾·d phones with the strongest signal, and rotate the remaining slots through other nearby phones from time to time. No extra messages are needed.
  • N1+N2 — diverse views (HyParView-style): keep a list of known phones besides the live links, swap parts of that list with neighbors now and then, and prefer phones whose own neighbors are not already reachable. That costs a small amount of extra traffic (neighbor lists).

Both keep at least one slot free so that a newly arriving phone can always connect (N3, newcomer reservation).

We vary how crowded the area is (density = phones within radio range: low 2–10, medium 10–50, high 50–200), the mesh size N (10 to 1000 phones), how people move, the mix of Android and iPhone (iPhones in the background are reachable only about 10 s per minute), and d. Each setting was run with 10 different random seeds; charts show the mean and a 95% confidence interval (whisker). If whiskers of the two strategies do not overlap, the difference is unlikely to be chance.

7560 simulation runs in 756 cells. Each run simulates 15 minutes; the first 2 minutes are ignored as warm-up (the mesh settles within about 1 minute, see the time-series chart below).

Run notes
Stage A run (all numbers simulated)
- Command: java -Xmx3g -cp 'build/run/*' meshlink.routingsim.runner.MainKt stage-a --threads 4 --seeds 10 --plan full
- Code: branch prototype/routing-sim, commit 87eff78 (simulator); report/README in aedc261.
- Started 2026-11-01 13:39 CET; 7,560 runs in 4,188 s wall (70 min) at 4 threads; sum of per-run wall 16,746 s; slowest run 59 s (high density, N=1000). Machine shared with a BLE bake-off (load 5-7).
- Pilot extrapolation was ~50 min; actual 70 min (contention). Under the ~3 h budget, so NO pruning: full grid, 10 seeds per cell.
- Grid: main 7,200 runs (9 density x N combos x 5 mobility x 2 platform mixes x d in {3,5,6,8} x 2 strategies x 10 seeds), Sybil 240, supervision-drop sensitivity 120.
- Skipped as impossible: medium density with N=10; high density with N=10 and N=50.
- Warm-up: timeseries.csv shows the largest connected component settles within ~60 s; metrics use minutes 2-15.

Colors:N0 strongest signalN1+N2 diverse views

What we found

All findings below are simulated, from the model described in the prototype README. They are not device measurements.

  1. N1+N2 (diverse views) gives a better-connected mesh in most settings and is never clearly worse, except in one corner. In the 360 settings of the main grid, N1+N2 had a clearly larger connected group in 216, N0 in 6, and 138 showed no clear difference. For mesh diameter (hops across the mesh) the count was 219 for N1+N2, 30 for N0 and 111 without a clear difference.
  2. At medium and high density, N1+N2 makes the mesh much "shorter". Example: medium density, 200 phones, d = 5, Android: diameter 15.1 ± 0.2 hops (N0) vs 10.4 ± 0.1 (N1+N2) in a static crowd; high density, 1000 phones: 31.8 ± 0.4 vs 16.4 ± 0.2. Fewer hops means less forwarding and lower delivery delay in later stages.
  3. With a small budget (d = 3) N0 falls apart, N1+N2 does not. Medium density, 200 phones, static crowd: largest connected group 0.41 ± 0.03 (N0) vs 0.97 ± 0.01 (N1+N2). With strongest-signal choice, close neighbors link to each other and form islands.
  4. Groups and corridors. When people walk in groups or in a line, N0 keeps more links inside the group or near neighbor, so it splits more: medium density, 1000 phones, corridor: 0.84 ± 0.01 (N0) vs 0.98 ± 0.00 (N1+N2).
  5. At low density the difference is small because radio range is the limit, not the choice of links. Most low-density settings show no clear difference; the six settings where N0 is clearly better are all low density with people walking (random waypoint) and larger d, and the gap there is about 0.01 (for example 0.954 ± 0.002 vs 0.940 ± 0.003).
  6. Cost. N1+N2 sends neighbor lists and shuffles: about 18–48 bytes per phone per second at d = 5 (up to ~150 at d = 8), at most 0.12% of link capacity — far below the 10% limit. It dials more at medium and high density, especially at d = 8 (medium density, 200 phones, walking, d = 8: 18.7 vs 15.1 dials per phone-minute), and fewer dials at low density. New phones with a peer in range get their first link quickly with both (median over settings 1.3 s for N0, 1.2 s for N1+N2; N1+N2 clearly faster in 303 of 360 settings, never clearly slower).
  7. Partition repair is mixed. When two separated groups come back into range, at low density N0 builds the first bridging link sooner (e.g. 2.0 ± 0.6 s vs 5.7 ± 1.8 s with 200 phones); at medium and high density the intervals overlap.
  8. Fake identities (Sybil). With 10% fakes, both strategies lose about 40% of honest link slots to fakes, but N1+N2 keeps the honest phones much better connected (medium density, static: 0.91 ± 0.02 vs 0.61 ± 0.05). With 30% fakes both collapse (honest connected group ≤ 0.05): neither strategy has a real defense; that needs separate protection (e.g. identity cost, peer scoring).
  9. Model sensitivity. If links drop less often on their own (average every 180 s or never, instead of 45 s), connection churn falls, but the ranking of the two strategies does not change in the tested settings.

Fake identities (Sybil attack)

An attacker runs many fake identities (here 10% or 30% of all nodes; one radio can hold up to 16 connections). Fakes advertise with a 10 dB stronger signal, connect to every honest phone they hear, never close links, and never forward anything. We measure how many honest link slots the fakes capture and whether the honest phones still form one mesh. N=200, d=5, Android. No defenses beyond the strategy itself are modeled.

Does the mesh settle? (warm-up check)

Largest connected group over the 15 simulated minutes for four example settings (seed 1). The mesh settles within about one minute; measurements start at 2 minutes (dashed line).

What the measures mean

MeasureUnitMeaningBetter
Largest connected group (lcc)share of nodesShare of online nodes that can reach each other over mesh links (1.0 = everyone is in one mesh).higher
Radio-reachable group (upper bound) (radioLcc)share of nodesSame, if every pair in radio range were linked: the best any strategy could do.context
Separate groups (components)countNumber of disconnected pieces the mesh falls into (single nodes count).lower
Mesh diameter (diameter)hopsLongest shortest path in the largest group, in hops. Lower means fewer hops to cross the mesh. Above 300 nodes this is a sampled lower bound.lower
Average path length (meanPathLen)hopsAverage hops between two nodes in the largest group.lower
Link slots in use (slotOccupancy)share of dShare of each node's connection budget d that holds a live link.context
Dials (dialsPerNodeMin)per node-minuteConnection attempts started per node per minute (radio and battery cost).lower
Dial failure rate (dialFailureRate)shareShare of connection attempts that failed.lower
Connection churn (linkUpsPerNodeMin)links up per node-minuteNew links established per node per minute.lower
Median link lifetime (linkLifetimeMedianS)sHow long a link typically lives.context
Control traffic (controlBytesPerNodeS)bytes per node-secondBytes spent on topology upkeep (neighbor lists, shuffles).lower
Control traffic share (controlShareOfCapacity)of link capacityControl bytes as a share of the link capacity the node has (decision limit: 10%).lower
Joiners connected within 60 s (joinConnected60s)shareShare of (re)joining nodes with a peer in range that got their first link within 60 s.higher
Time to first link (median) (joinMedianS)sMedian time from joining until the first mesh link.lower
Partition repair: first bridging link (mergeFirstLinkS)sAfter two separated groups come back into radio range: time until the first link between them.lower
Partition repair: mesh recovered (mergeLccRecoverS)sTime until the largest group again holds 90% of what radio range allows.lower
Honest slots held by fake identities (sybilSlotShare)shareShare of honest nodes' links that go to fake identities.lower
Peer-table entries that are fake (sybilPeerTableShare)shareShare of honest nodes' candidate lists (peer table or passive view) taken by fake identities.lower
Peers in radio range (measured) (peersInRange)countMeasured average number of online peers within radio range.context
